<hr />
<span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/Government_of_Australia" title="Government of Australia"><img alt="Government of Australia" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg/40px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg.png" decoding="async" width="40" height="31" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg/60px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg/80px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="397" /></a></span></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar" style="text-align:center;"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:John_Howard_sidebar" title="Template:John Howard sidebar"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:John_Howard_sidebar" title="Template talk:John Howard sidebar"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:John_Howard_sidebar&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>John Winston Howard</b> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Member_of_the_Order_of_Merit" class="mw-redirect" title="Member of the Order of Merit">OM</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Companion_of_the_Order_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Companion of the Order of Australia">AC</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Star_of_the_Solomon_Islands" title="Star of the Solomon Islands">SSI</a></span> (born 26 July 1939) is an Australian former politician who served as the 25th <a href="/wiki/Prime_minister_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime minister of Australia">prime minister of Australia</a>, from 1996 to 2007, holding office as leader of the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia" title="Liberal Party of Australia">Liberal Party of Australia</a>. His eleven-year tenure as prime minister is the second-longest in Australian history, behind only Sir <a href="/wiki/Robert_Menzies" title="Robert Menzies">Robert Menzies</a>. Howard previously served as the 29th <a href="/wiki/Treasurer_of_Australia" title="Treasurer of Australia">treasurer of Australia</a> from 1977 to 1983 under Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_Fraser" title="Malcolm Fraser">Malcolm Fraser</a>. He has also been the oldest living Australian prime minister since the death of <a href="/wiki/Bob_Hawke" title="Bob Hawke">Bob Hawke</a> in May 2019.
</p><p>Howard was born in <a href="/wiki/Sydney" title="Sydney">Sydney</a> and studied law at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Sydney" title="University of Sydney">University of Sydney</a>. He was a commercial lawyer before entering parliament. A former federal president of the <a href="/wiki/Young_Liberals_(Australia)" title="Young Liberals (Australia)">Young Liberals</a>, he first stood for office at the <a href="/wiki/1968_New_South_Wales_state_election" title="1968 New South Wales state election">1968 New South Wales state election</a>, but lost narrowly. At the <a href="/wiki/1974_Australian_federal_election" title="1974 Australian federal election">1974 federal election</a>, Howard was elected as a <a href="/wiki/Member_of_parliament" title="Member of parliament">member of parliament</a> (MP) for the <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Bennelong" title="Division of Bennelong">division of Bennelong</a>. He was promoted to cabinet in 1977, and later in the year replaced <a href="/wiki/Phillip_Lynch" title="Phillip Lynch">Phillip Lynch</a> as <a href="/wiki/Treasurer_of_Australia" title="Treasurer of Australia">treasurer of Australia</a>, remaining in that position until the defeat of <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_Fraser" title="Malcolm Fraser">Malcolm Fraser</a>'s government at the <a href="/wiki/1983_Australian_federal_election" title="1983 Australian federal election">1983 election</a>. In 1985, Howard was elected leader of the Liberal Party for the first time, thus replacing <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Peacock" title="Andrew Peacock">Andrew Peacock</a> as <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_(Australia)" title="Leader of the Opposition (Australia)">Leader of the Opposition</a>. He led the <a href="/wiki/Coalition_(Australia)" title="Coalition (Australia)">Liberal–National coalition</a> to the <a href="/wiki/1987_Australian_federal_election" title="1987 Australian federal election">1987 federal election</a>, but lost to <a href="/wiki/Bob_Hawke" title="Bob Hawke">Bob Hawke</a>'s Labor government, and was removed from the leadership in 1989. Remaining a key figure in the party, Howard was re-elected leader in 1995, replacing <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Downer" title="Alexander Downer">Alexander Downer</a>, and subsequently led the Coalition to a <a href="/wiki/Landslide_victory" title="Landslide victory">landslide victory</a> at the <a href="/wiki/1996_Australian_federal_election" title="1996 Australian federal election">1996 federal election</a>.
</p><p>In his first term, Howard introduced <a href="/wiki/Gun_laws_in_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Gun laws in Australia">reformed gun laws</a> in response to the <a href="/wiki/Port_Arthur_massacre_(Australia)" title="Port Arthur massacre (Australia)">Port Arthur massacre</a>, and controversially implemented <a href="/wiki/Goods_and_Services_Tax_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Goods and Services Tax (Australia)">a nationwide value-added tax</a>, breaking a pre-election promise. The <a href="/wiki/Howard_government" title="Howard government">Howard government</a> called a <a href="/wiki/Snap_election" title="Snap election">snap election</a> for <a href="/wiki/1998_Australian_federal_election" title="1998 Australian federal election">October 1998</a>, which they won, albeit with a greatly reduced majority. Going into the <a href="/wiki/2001_Australian_federal_election" title="2001 Australian federal election">2001 election</a>, the Coalition trailed behind Labor in <a href="/wiki/Opinion_polling" class="mw-redirect" title="Opinion polling">opinion polling</a>. However, in a campaign dominated by national security, Howard introduced <a href="/wiki/Pacific_Solution" title="Pacific Solution">changes to Australia's immigration system</a> to deter <a href="/wiki/Asylum_seekers" class="mw-redirect" title="Asylum seekers">asylum seekers</a> from entering the country, and pledged military assistance to the United States following the <a href="/wiki/September_11_attacks" title="September 11 attacks">September 11 attacks</a>. Due to this, Howard won widespread support, and his government would be narrowly re-elected.
</p><p>In Howard's third term in office, Australia contributed troops to the <a href="/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)" title="War in Afghanistan (2001–2021)">War in Afghanistan</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Iraq_War" title="Iraq War">Iraq War</a>, and led the <a href="/wiki/International_Force_for_East_Timor" class="mw-redirect" title="International Force for East Timor">International Force for East Timor</a>. The Coalition would be re-elected once more at the <a href="/wiki/2004_Australian_federal_election" title="2004 Australian federal election">2004 federal election</a>. In his final term in office, his government introduced industrial relations reforms known as  <a href="/wiki/WorkChoices" title="WorkChoices">WorkChoices</a>, which proved controversial and unpopular with the public. The Howard government was defeated at the <a href="/wiki/2007_Australian_federal_election" title="2007 Australian federal election">2007 federal election</a>, with the Labor Party's <a href="/wiki/Kevin_Rudd" title="Kevin Rudd">Kevin Rudd</a> succeeding him as prime minister. Howard also lost his own seat of Bennelong at the election to <a href="/wiki/Maxine_McKew" title="Maxine McKew">Maxine McKew</a>, becoming only the second prime minister to do so, after <a href="/wiki/Stanley_Bruce" title="Stanley Bruce">Stanley Bruce</a> at the <a href="/wiki/1929_Australian_federal_election" title="1929 Australian federal election">1929 election</a>. Following this loss, Howard retired from politics, but has remained active in political discourse.
</p><p>Howard's government presided over a sustained period of economic growth and a large "<a href="/wiki/Mining_in_Australia" title="Mining in Australia">mining boom</a>", and significantly reduced government debt by the time he left office. He was known for his broad appeal to voters across the political spectrum, and commanded a diverse base of supporters, colloquially referred to as his "<a href="/wiki/Battler_(underdog)" title="Battler (underdog)">battlers</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> Retrospectively, ratings of Howard's premiership have been polarised. His critics have admonished him for involving Australia in the Iraq War, his policies regarding asylum seekers, and his economic agenda.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Nonetheless, he has been frequently <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_prime_ministers_of_Australia" title="Historical rankings of prime ministers of Australia">ranked within the upper-tier</a> of Australian prime ministers by political experts and the general public.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_and_personal_life">Early and personal life</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Howard_boy.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Photograph of John Howard as a boy, taken in the 1940s" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/John_Howard_boy.jpg/150px-John_Howard_boy.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="232" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/John_Howard_boy.jpg/224px-John_Howard_boy.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/d4/John_Howard_boy.jpg 2x" data-file-width="282" data-file-height="437" /></a><figcaption>Young Howard, 1940s</figcaption></figure>
<p>Howard is the fourth son of Mona (<i>née</i> Kell) and <a href="/wiki/Lyall_Howard" title="Lyall Howard">Lyall Howard</a>, who married in 1925. Howard was also known as "Jack" in his youth.<sup id="cite_ref-SMH_Canterbury_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SMH_Canterbury-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> His older brothers were Walter (1926–2019), Stanley (1930–2014) and <a href="/wiki/Bob_Howard_(academic)" class="mw-redirect" title="Bob Howard (academic)">Robert</a> (b. 1936). Lyall Howard was an admirer of <a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGarran200410_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGarran200410-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Howard's ancestors were English, Scottish, and Irish.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEErringtonVan_Onselen20072–4_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEErringtonVan_Onselen20072–4-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> He is descended from <a href="/wiki/Convicts_in_Australia" title="Convicts in Australia">convict</a> William Tooley, who was <a href="/wiki/Penal_transportation" title="Penal transportation">transported</a> to New South Wales in 1816 for stealing a watch.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Howard was born and raised in the <a href="/wiki/Sydney" title="Sydney">Sydney</a> suburb of <a href="/wiki/Earlwood,_New_South_Wales" title="Earlwood, New South Wales">Earlwood</a>, in a <a href="/wiki/Methodist" class="mw-redirect" title="Methodist">Methodist</a> family; the site of his family home is now a <a href="/wiki/KFC" title="KFC">KFC</a> restaurant.<sup id="cite_ref-Kelly_bio_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kelly_bio-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> His mother had been an office worker until her marriage, while his father and his paternal grandfather, Walter Howard, were both veterans of the <a href="/wiki/First_Australian_Imperial_Force" title="First Australian Imperial Force">First Australian Imperial Force</a> in the <a href="/wiki/First_World_War" class="mw-redirect" title="First World War">First World War</a>. They also ran two <a href="/wiki/Dulwich_Hill,_New_South_Wales" class="mw-redirect" title="Dulwich Hill, New South Wales">Dulwich Hill</a> petrol stations where Howard worked as a boy.<sup id="cite_ref-SMH_Petrol_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SMH_Petrol-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> In 1955, when Howard was aged 16, his father died, leaving his mother to take care of him.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Howard suffered a hearing impairment in his youth, leaving him with a slight <a href="/wiki/Speech_disorder" title="Speech disorder">speech impediment</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-transcript-lab_opening_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-transcript-lab_opening-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability"><span title="The material near this tag failed verification of its source citation(s). (June 2021)">failed verification</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> and he continues to wear a hearing aid. It also influenced him in subtle ways, limiting his early academic performance; encouraging a reliance on an excellent memory; and in his mind ruling out becoming a <a href="/wiki/Barrister" title="Barrister">barrister</a> as a likely career.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEErringtonVan_Onselen200721,_35_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEErringtonVan_Onselen200721,_35-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Howard attended Earlwood Primary School and <a href="/wiki/Canterbury_Boys%27_High_School" title="Canterbury Boys&#39; High School">Canterbury Boys' High School</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-SMH_Canterbury_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SMH_Canterbury-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> He won a citizenship prize in his final year at Earlwood (presented by local politician <a href="/wiki/Eric_Willis" title="Eric Willis">Eric Willis</a>), and subsequently represented his secondary school at debating as well as <a href="/wiki/Cricket" title="Cricket">cricket</a> and <a href="/wiki/Rugby_union" title="Rugby union">rugby union</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-The_Sports_Factor_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Sports_Factor-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Cricket remained a lifelong hobby.<sup id="cite_ref-Kelly_bio_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kelly_bio-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> In his final year at school he took part in a radio show hosted by <a href="/wiki/Jack_Davey" title="Jack Davey">Jack Davey</a>, <i>Give It a Go</i>, broadcast on the commercial radio station, <a href="/wiki/2GB" title="2GB">2GB</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-young_howard_recording_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-young_howard_recording-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> After gaining his <a href="/wiki/Leaving_Certificate" class="mw-redirect" title="Leaving Certificate">Leaving Certificate</a>, he studied law at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Sydney" title="University of Sydney">University of Sydney</a>, graduating with a <a href="/wiki/Bachelor_of_Laws" title="Bachelor of Laws">Bachelor of Laws</a> degree in 1962.<sup id="cite_ref-SMH_Canterbury_10-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SMH_Canterbury-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Howard began working for the firm of Stephen Jaques and Stephen as a junior solicitor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEErringtonVan_Onselen200735_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEErringtonVan_Onselen200735-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> In 1964, he took a trip around the world, visiting Britain, Europe, Israel, India, and Singapore.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEErringtonVan_Onselen200739_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEErringtonVan_Onselen200739-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> After returning to Sydney in 1965, he began working for <a href="/wiki/Clayton_Utz" title="Clayton Utz">Clayton Utz</a>, but "lacked the university grades and the social connections to be on track for a partnership". He subsequently moved to a smaller firm, which became Truman, Nelson and Howard after he was made a partner.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEErringtonVan_Onselen200740_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEErringtonVan_Onselen200740-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Howard married fellow Liberal Party member <a href="/wiki/Janette_Howard" title="Janette Howard">Janette Parker</a> in 1971, with whom he had three children: Melanie (1974), Tim (1977) and Richard (1980).<sup id="cite_ref-NAA_JanetteHoward_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NAA_JanetteHoward-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> John and Janette are <a href="/wiki/Christianity" title="Christianity">Christians</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_political_career">Early political career</span></h2>
<p>Howard joined the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia" title="Liberal Party of Australia">Liberal Party</a> in 1957. He was a member of the party's New South Wales state executive and was federal president of the <a href="/wiki/Young_Liberals_(Australia)" title="Young Liberals (Australia)">Young Liberals</a> (the party youth organisation) from 1962 to 1964.<sup id="cite_ref-young_libs_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-young_libs-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Howard supported <a href="/wiki/Military_history_of_Australia_during_the_Vietnam_War" title="Military history of Australia during the Vietnam War">Australia's involvement</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Vietnam_War" title="Vietnam War">Vietnam War</a>, although has since said there were "aspects of it that could have been handled and explained differently".<sup id="cite_ref-4corners_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4corners-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the <a href="/wiki/1963_Australian_federal_election" title="1963 Australian federal election">1963 federal election</a>, Howard acted as campaign manager for <a href="/wiki/Tom_Hughes_(Australian_politician)" title="Tom Hughes (Australian politician)">Tom Hughes</a> in his local seat of <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Parkes_(1901-69)" class="mw-redirect" title="Division of Parkes (1901-69)">Parkes</a>. Hughes went on to defeat the 20-year Labor incumbent, <a href="/wiki/Les_Haylen" title="Les Haylen">Les Haylen</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> In mid-1964, Howard travelled to <a href="/wiki/London" title="London">London</a> to work and travel for a period. He volunteered for the <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK)" title="Conservative Party (UK)">Conservative Party</a> in the electorate of <a href="/wiki/Holborn_and_St_Pancras_South_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Holborn and St Pancras South (UK Parliament constituency)">Holborn and St Pancras South</a> at the <a href="/wiki/1964_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1964 United Kingdom general election">1964 UK general election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoward201035-36_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoward201035-36-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> In 1967, with the support of party power brokers <a href="/wiki/John_Carrick_(Australian_politician)" title="John Carrick (Australian politician)">John Carrick</a> and <a href="/wiki/Eric_Willis" title="Eric Willis">Eric Willis</a>, Howard was endorsed as candidate for the marginal suburban state seat of <a href="/wiki/Electoral_district_of_Drummoyne" title="Electoral district of Drummoyne">Drummoyne</a>, held by Labor's <a href="/wiki/Reg_Coady" title="Reg Coady">Reg Coady</a>. Howard's mother sold the family home in <a href="/wiki/Earlwood,_New_South_Wales" title="Earlwood, New South Wales">Earlwood</a> and rented a house with him at <a href="/wiki/Five_Dock,_New_South_Wales" title="Five Dock, New South Wales">Five Dock</a>, a suburb within the electorate. At <a href="/wiki/1968_New_South_Wales_state_election" title="1968 New South Wales state election">the election in February 1968</a>, in which the incumbent state Liberal government was returned to office, Howard narrowly lost to Coady, despite campaigning vigorously.<sup id="cite_ref-Drummoyne_1968_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Drummoyne_1968-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the <a href="/wiki/1974_Australian_federal_election" title="1974 Australian federal election">1974 federal election</a>, Howard successfully contested the <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Bennelong" title="Division of Bennelong">Division of Bennelong</a>, located in suburban Sydney.<sup id="cite_ref-Howard_aph_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Howard_aph-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> The election saw the return of the <a href="/wiki/Gough_Whitlam" title="Gough Whitlam">Gough Whitlam</a>-led Labor government. Howard supported <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_Fraser" title="Malcolm Fraser">Malcolm Fraser</a> for the leadership of the Liberal Party against <a href="/wiki/Billy_Snedden" title="Billy Snedden">Billy Snedden</a> following the 1974 election.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKelly1994101_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKelly1994101-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> When Fraser won office at the <a href="/wiki/1975_Australian_federal_election" title="1975 Australian federal election">1975 federal election</a>, Howard was appointed <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Competition_Policy_and_Consumer_Affairs_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister for Competition Policy and Consumer Affairs (Australia)">Minister for Business and Consumer Affairs</a>, a position in which he served until 1977.<sup id="cite_ref-NMA_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NMA-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> At this stage, he followed the <a href="/wiki/Protectionist" class="mw-redirect" title="Protectionist">protectionist</a> and pro-regulation stance of Fraser and the Liberal Party.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKelly1994101–103_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKelly1994101–103-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Federal_Treasurer">Federal Treasurer</span></h2>
<p>In December 1977, aged 38, Howard was appointed <a href="/wiki/Treasurer_of_Australia" title="Treasurer of Australia">Treasurer</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Howard_aph_31-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Howard_aph-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> in place of <a href="/wiki/Phillip_Lynch" title="Phillip Lynch">Phillip Lynch</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-NMA_33-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NMA-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> He was the youngest Treasurer since <a href="/wiki/Chris_Watson" title="Chris Watson">Chris Watson</a> in 1904. Fraser said in his memoirs that he appointed him despite his limited experience because "he was bright and he got across a brief well, and he was a good manager".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFraserSimons2011351_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFraserSimons2011351-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
During his five years in the position, Howard became an adherent of <a href="/wiki/Market_economics" class="mw-redirect" title="Market economics">free-market economics</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKelly1994102_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKelly1994102-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> which was challenging economic orthodoxies in place for most of the century.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKelly199450–53_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKelly199450–53-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> He came to favour tax reform including broad-based taxation (later the <a href="/wiki/Goods_and_Services_Tax_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Goods and Services Tax (Australia)">GST</a>), a freer industrial system including the dismantling of the centralised wage-fixing system, the abolition of compulsory <a href="/wiki/Trade_unionism" class="mw-redirect" title="Trade unionism">trade unionism</a>, privatisation and deregulation.<sup id="cite_ref-Kelly_bio_14-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kelly_bio-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1978, the Fraser government instigated the Campbell Committee to investigate financial system reforms.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBell200421_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBell200421-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Howard supported the Campbell report, but adopted an incremental approach with Cabinet, as there was wide opposition to deregulation within the government and the treasury.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBell200421_38-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBell200421-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKelly199478_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKelly199478-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> The process of reform began before the committee reported 2<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1154941027">.mw-parser-output .frac{white-space:nowrap}.mw-parser-output .frac .num,.mw-parser-output .frac .den{font-size:80%;line-height:0;vertical-align:super}.mw-parser-output .frac .den{vertical-align:sub}.mw-parser-output .sr-only{border:0;clip:rect(0,0,0,0);clip-path:polygon(0px 0px,0px 0px,0px 0px);height:1px;margin:-1px;overflow:hidden;padding:0;position:absolute;width:1px}</style><span class="frac" role="math"><span class="num">1</span>&#8260;<span class="den">2</span></span> years later, with the introduction of the tender system for the sale of Treasury notes in 1979, and Treasury bonds in 1982. <a href="/wiki/Ian_Macfarlane_(economist)" title="Ian Macfarlane (economist)">Ian Macfarlane</a> described these reforms as "second only in importance to the float of the Australian dollar in 1983."<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> In 1981, Howard proposed a broad-based indirect tax with compensatory cuts in personal rates; however, cabinet rejected it citing both inflationary and political reasons.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKelly199449_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKelly199449-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> After the free-marketeers or "drys" of the Liberals challenged the protectionist policies of <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Employment_and_Workplace_Relations_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister for Employment and Workplace Relations (Australia)">Minister for Industry and Commerce</a> <a href="/wiki/Phillip_Lynch" title="Phillip Lynch">Phillip Lynch</a>, they shifted their loyalties to Howard. Following an unsuccessful leadership challenge by <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Peacock" title="Andrew Peacock">Andrew Peacock</a> to unseat Fraser as prime minister, Howard was <a href="/wiki/1982_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill" title="1982 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill">elected deputy leader</a> of the Liberal Party in April 1982. His election depended largely on the support of the "drys", and he became the party's champion of the growing free-market lobby.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKelly199449–50_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKelly199449–50-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The economic crises of the early 1980s brought Howard into conflict with the <a href="/wiki/Keynesian" class="mw-redirect" title="Keynesian">Keynesian</a> Fraser. As the economy headed towards the worst recession since the 1930s, Fraser pushed an expansionary fiscal position much to Howard's and Treasury's horror. With his authority as treasurer being flouted, Howard considered resigning in July 1982, but, after discussions with his wife and senior advisor <a href="/wiki/John_Hewson" title="John Hewson">John Hewson</a>, he decided to "tough it out".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKelly199450–53_37-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKelly199450–53-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> The 1982 wages explosion—wages rose 16 per cent across the country—resulted in <a href="/wiki/Stagflation" title="Stagflation">stagflation</a>; unemployment touched double-digits and inflation peaked at 12.5% (official interest rates peaked at 21%).<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Fraser government with Howard as Treasurer lost the <a href="/wiki/1983_Australian_federal_election" title="1983 Australian federal election">1983 election</a> to the Labor Party led by <a href="/wiki/Bob_Hawke" title="Bob Hawke">Bob Hawke</a>. Over the course of the 1980s, the Liberal Party came to accept the free-market policies that Fraser had resisted and Howard had espoused. Policies included low protection, decentralisation of wage fixation, financial deregulation, a broadly based indirect tax, and the rejection of counter-cyclical fiscal policy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKelly1994232_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKelly1994232-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Opposition">Opposition</span></h2>
<p>Following the defeat of the Fraser government and Fraser's subsequent resignation from parliament, Howard contested the Liberal leadership against <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Peacock" title="Andrew Peacock">Andrew Peacock</a>, <a href="/wiki/1983_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_election" title="1983 Liberal Party of Australia leadership election">losing 36–20</a>. However, he was re-elected as deputy leader. The Liberal Party were again defeated by Labor at the early <a href="/wiki/1984_Australian_federal_election" title="1984 Australian federal election">1984 election</a>. In 1985, as Labor's position in opinion polls improved, Peacock's popularity sank and Howard's profile rose. Leadership speculation persisted, and Peacock said he would no longer accept Howard as deputy unless he offered assurances that he would not challenge for the leadership. Following Howard's refusal to offer such an assurance, Peacock sought, in September 1985, to replace him with <a href="/wiki/John_Moore_(Australian_politician)" title="John Moore (Australian politician)">John Moore</a> as deputy leader.<sup id="cite_ref-Barclay_264_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Barclay_264-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> The party room re-elected Howard as deputy on 5 September 38 votes to 31, which Peacock treated as a <a href="/wiki/Vote_of_no_confidence" class="mw-redirect" title="Vote of no confidence">vote of no confidence</a> in his leadership. He subsequently called <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill,_1985" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill, 1985">a leadership ballot</a>, which he chose not to contest. Howard defeated <a href="/wiki/Jim_Carlton" title="Jim Carlton">Jim Carlton</a> by 57 votes to six, thus becoming Leader of the Opposition.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKelly1994192_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKelly1994192-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Leader_of_the_Opposition_.281985.E2.80.931989.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Leader_of_the_Opposition_(1985–1989)">Leader of the Opposition (1985–1989)</span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="New_economic_policy">New economic policy</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1126788409"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1045330069"><table class="sidebar sidebar-collapse nomobile nowraplinks"><tbody><tr><td class="sidebar-pretitle">This article is part of <a href="/wiki/Category:Conservatism_in_Australia" title="Category:Conservatism in Australia">a series</a> on</td></tr><tr><th class="sidebar-title-with-pretitle" style="background:#154E9D"><a href="/wiki/Conservatism_in_Australia" title="Conservatism in Australia"><span style="color:white;">Conservatism in Australia</span></a></th></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-image"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg/100px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg.png" decoding="async" width="100" height="78" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg/150px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg/200px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="397" /></a></span></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-content">

<div class="sidebar-list mw-collapsible mw-collapsed"><div class="sidebar-list-title" style="background:transparent;border-top:1px solid #aaa;text-align:center;">Jurists</div><div class="sidebar-list-content mw-collapsible-content hlist">
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/James_Allan_(law_professor)" title="James Allan (law professor)">Allan</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Edmund_Barton" title="Edmund Barton">Barton</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Ian_Callinan" title="Ian Callinan">Callinan</a></li>
