</p>
<ul><li><i><a href="/wiki/The_Argus_(Melbourne)" title="The Argus (Melbourne)">The Argus</a></i></li></ul></div></div></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-content">
<div class="sidebar-list mw-collapsible mw-collapsed"><div class="sidebar-list-title" style="background:transparent;border-top:1px solid #aaa;text-align:center;">Related topics</div><div class="sidebar-list-content mw-collapsible-content hlist">
<ul><li>Liberal Party factions
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Centre_Right_(Liberal_Party_of_Australia)" title="Centre Right (Liberal Party of Australia)">Centre Right</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/National_Right_(Liberal_Party_of_Australia)" title="National Right (Liberal Party of Australia)">National Right</a></li></ul></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Liberal_conservatism" title="Liberal conservatism">Liberal conservatism</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Liberalism_in_Australia" title="Liberalism in Australia">Liberalism in Australia</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Politics_of_Australia" title="Politics of Australia">Politics of Australia</a></li></ul></div></div></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-below plainlist">
<ul><li><span class="noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/DodgerBlue_flag_waving.svg/15px-DodgerBlue_flag_waving.svg.png" decoding="async" width="15" height="16" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/DodgerBlue_flag_waving.svg/22px-DodgerBlue_flag_waving.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/DodgerBlue_flag_waving.svg/30px-DodgerBlue_flag_waving.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="249" data-file-height="268" /></span></span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Conservatism" title="Portal:Conservatism">Conservatism&#32;portal</a></li>
<li><span class="mw-image-border noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="flag" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/b9/Flag_of_Australia.svg/16px-Flag_of_Australia.svg.png" decoding="async" width="16" height="8" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/b9/Flag_of_Australia.svg/24px-Flag_of_Australia.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/b9/Flag_of_Australia.svg/32px-Flag_of_Australia.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1280" data-file-height="640" /></span></span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Australia" title="Portal:Australia">Australia&#32;portal</a></li></ul></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Howard was in effect the Liberal party's first pro-market leader in the conservative <a href="/wiki/Coalition_(Australia)" title="Coalition (Australia)">Coalition</a> and spent the next two years working to revise Liberal policy away from that of Fraser's.<sup id="cite_ref-AJPH2008p279_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AJPH2008p279-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> In his own words he was an "economic radical" and a <a href="/wiki/Social_conservatism" title="Social conservatism">social conservative</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarkus200185–89_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarkus200185–89-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Referring to the pro-market liberalism of the 1980s, Howard said in July 1986 that "The times will suit me".<sup id="cite_ref-SMH20030818_TimesDoSuitHim_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SMH20030818_TimesDoSuitHim-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> That year the economy was seen to be in crisis with a 40% devaluation of the Australian dollar, a marked increase in the current account deficit and the loss of the Federal Government's triple A rating.<sup id="cite_ref-SMH20030818_TimesDoSuitHim_51-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SMH20030818_TimesDoSuitHim-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> In response to the economic circumstances, Howard persistently attacked the Labor government and offered his free-market reform agenda.<sup id="cite_ref-SMH20030818_TimesDoSuitHim_51-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SMH20030818_TimesDoSuitHim-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> Support for the Labor Party and Hawke strengthened in 1985 and 1986 and Howard's approval ratings dropped in the face of infighting between Howard and Peacock supporters, a "public manifestation of disunity" over policy positions, and questions over Howard's leadership.<sup id="cite_ref-Barclay_264_45-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Barclay_264-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Hawke called the <a href="/wiki/1987_Australian_federal_election" title="1987 Australian federal election">1987 federal election</a> six months early. In addition to the Howard–Peacock rivalry, Queensland National Party criticism of the federal Liberal and National leadership<sup id="cite_ref-AJPH2008p279_49-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AJPH2008p279-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> culminated in longtime <a href="/wiki/Queensland_Premier" class="mw-redirect" title="Queensland Premier">Queensland Premier</a> <a href="/wiki/Joh_Bjelke-Petersen" title="Joh Bjelke-Petersen">Joh Bjelke-Petersen</a> making a bid to become prime minister himself—the "<a href="/wiki/Joh_for_Canberra" title="Joh for Canberra">Joh for Canberra</a>" campaign. Keating campaigned against Howard's proposed tax changes forcing Howard to admit a <a href="/wiki/Double_counting_(accounting)" title="Double counting (accounting)">double-counting</a> in the proposal,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKelly1994427,_457_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKelly1994427,_457-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> and emphasising to the electorate that the package would mean at that stage undisclosed cuts to government services. 
</p><p>Howard was not helped when the federal Nationals broke off the Coalition agreement in support of the "Joh for Canberra" push, which led to a large number of three-cornered contests. Bjelke-Petersen abandoned his bid for prime minister a month before the election, however, the damage had already been done. Additionally, a number of swing voters outside Queensland were alarmed at the prospect of Bjelke-Petersen holding the balance of power, and voted for Labor to ensure that the Liberals and Nationals would be defeated. As a result, the Hawke government was handily reelected, winning the most seats that Labor had ever won in an election.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Social_agenda">Social agenda</span></h4>
<p>In his social agenda, Howard promoted the traditional family and was antipathetic to the promotion of <a href="/wiki/Multiculturalism" title="Multiculturalism">multiculturalism</a> at the expense of a shared Australian identity.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKelly1994419_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKelly1994419-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> The controversial immigration policy, <i><a href="/wiki/One_Australia_policy" class="mw-redirect" title="One Australia policy">One Australia</a></i>, outlined a vision of "one nation and one future" and opposed multiculturalism.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarkus200185–89_50-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarkus200185–89-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Howard publicly suggested that to support "social cohesion" the rate of Asian immigration be "slowed down a little".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEErringtonVan_Onselen2007157_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEErringtonVan_Onselen2007157-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> The comments divided opinion within the Coalition, and undermined Howard's standing amongst Liberal party figures including federal and state Ministers, intellectual opinion makers, business leaders, and within the Asia Pacific. Three Liberal MPs <a href="/wiki/Crossed_the_floor" class="mw-redirect" title="Crossed the floor">crossed the floor</a> and two abstained in response to a motion put forward by Prime Minister Hawke to affirm that race or ethnicity would not be used as immigrant selection criteria. Many Liberals later nominated the issue as instrumental in Howard subsequently losing the leadership in 1989.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKelly1994427–428_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKelly1994427–428-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> In a 1995 newspaper article (and in 2002 as prime minister), Howard recanted his 1988 remarks on curbing Asian immigration.<sup id="cite_ref-Aus_spice_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Aus_spice-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWard1995_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWard1995-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In line with "One Australia's" rejection of Aboriginal land rights, Howard said the idea of an Aboriginal treaty was "repugnant to the ideals of One Australia"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarkus200185–89_50-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarkus200185–89-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> and commented "I don't think it is wrong, racist, immoral or anything, for a country to say 'we will decide what the cultural identity and the cultural destiny of this country will be and nobody else."<sup id="cite_ref-SMH_cricket_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SMH_cricket-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Howard is opposed to abortion and voted against the <a href="/wiki/RU-486" class="mw-redirect" title="RU-486">RU-486</a> abortion drug being legalised.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Loss_of_the_leadership">Loss of the leadership</span></h3>
<p>As the <a href="/wiki/Early_1990s_recession" title="Early 1990s recession">country's economic position worsened</a> in 1989, public opinion moved away from Labor, however there was no firm opinion poll lead for Howard or the Coalition.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKelly1994470_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKelly1994470-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> In February, Liberal Party president and prominent businessman, <a href="/wiki/John_Elliott_(businessman)" title="John Elliott (businessman)">John Elliott</a>, said confidentially to Andrew Peacock that he would support him in a leadership challenge against Howard,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKelly1994427,_457_52-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKelly1994427,_457-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> and in May a <a href="/wiki/1989_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill" title="1989 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill">surprise leadership coup</a> was launched, ousting Howard as Liberal leader. When asked that day whether he could become Liberal leader again, Howard likened it to <i>"Lazarus with a triple bypass"</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> The loss of the Liberal Party leadership to Peacock deeply affected Howard, who admitted he would occasionally drink too much.<sup id="cite_ref-news_drunk_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-news_drunk-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Declining Peacock's offer of Shadow Education, Howard went to the backbench and a new period of party disunity ensued which was highlighted by a Four Corners episode detailing the coup against Howard.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following the Coalition's <a href="/wiki/1990_Australian_federal_election" title="1990 Australian federal election">1990 election</a> loss, Howard considered challenging Peacock for the leadership, but didn't have enough support for a bid. Ultimately, Peacock resigned and was replaced with Howard's former staffer <a href="/wiki/John_Hewson" title="John Hewson">John Hewson</a> who defeated <a href="/wiki/Peter_Reith" title="Peter Reith">Peter Reith</a>; Peacock supported Hewson as a symbol of <a href="/wiki/Generational_change" class="mw-redirect" title="Generational change">generational change</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> Howard was a supporter of Hewson's economic program, with a <a href="/wiki/Goods_and_Services_Tax_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Goods and Services Tax (Australia)">Goods and Services Tax</a> (GST) as its centrepiece.  Howard was Shadow Minister for Industrial relations and oversaw Jobsback section of Fightback. After Hewson lost the "unloseable" <a href="/wiki/1993_Australian_federal_election" title="1993 Australian federal election">1993 election</a> to <a href="/wiki/Paul_Keating" title="Paul Keating">Paul Keating</a>, Howard unsuccessfully <a href="/wiki/1993_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill" title="1993 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill">challenged</a> Hewson for the leadership. In 1994, he was again passed over for the leadership, which went to <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Downer" title="Alexander Downer">Alexander Downer</a>. Hewson had pledged to resign if defeated in 1993 but did not resign to block Howard from succeeding him.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Leader_of_the_Opposition_.281995.E2.80.931996.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Leader_of_the_Opposition_(1995–1996)">Leader of the Opposition (1995–1996)</span></h3>
<p>In January 1995, leaked internal Liberal Party polling showed that with gaffe-prone Downer as leader, the Coalition had slim chance of holding its marginal seats in the next election, let alone of winning government. Media speculation of a leadership spill ended when, on 26 January 1995, Downer resigned as Liberal Leader and Howard was <a href="/wiki/1995_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill" title="1995 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill">elected</a> unopposed to replace him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWard1995_58-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWard1995-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Coalition_(Australia)" title="Coalition (Australia)">Coalition</a> subsequently opened a large lead over Labor in most opinion polls, and Howard overtook <a href="/wiki/Paul_Keating" title="Paul Keating">Paul Keating</a> as preferred prime minister. Hoping to avoid a repeat of mistakes made at the 1993 election, Howard revised his earlier statements against <a href="/wiki/Medicare_(Australia)" title="Medicare (Australia)">Medicare</a> and Asian immigration, describing Australia as "a unique intersection between Europe, North America and Asia".<sup id="cite_ref-4corners_28-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4corners-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Aus_spice_57-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Aus_spice-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> This allowed Howard to campaign on a "small-target" strategy. He focused on the economy and memory of the early 1990s recession, and on the longevity of the Labor government, which in 1996 had been in power for 13 years. In May 1995, Howard also pledged that the GST would not be implemented by the Liberal Party as, the since retired, Hewson's defeat in 1993 was a rejection of the GST.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister">Prime Minister</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Howard_(cropped).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Photograph of John Howard, the Prime Minister of Australia, taken in June 1997" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/John_Howard_%28cropped%29.jpg/170px-John_Howard_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="218" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/John_Howard_%28cropped%29.jpg/255px-John_Howard_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/John_Howard_%28cropped%29.jpg/340px-John_Howard_%28cropped%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="813" data-file-height="1044" /></a><figcaption>Howard in June 1997, just over a year after becoming prime minister</figcaption></figure>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Howard_government" title="Howard government">Howard government</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="First_term">First term</span></h3>
<p>By the time the writs were issued for the <a href="/wiki/1996_Australian_federal_election" title="1996 Australian federal election">1996 election</a>, the Coalition had been well ahead of Labor in opinion polls for over a year. The consensus of most opinion polls was that Howard would be the next prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With the support of many traditionally Labor voters—dubbed "<a href="/wiki/Aussie_battler" class="mw-redirect" title="Aussie battler">Howard battlers</a>"—Howard and the Liberal-National Coalition swept to power on the back of a 29-seat swing. This was the second-worst defeat of an incumbent government since Federation. The Coalition picked up a five per cent swing, taking 13 seats away from Labor in New South Wales, and winning all but two seats in Queensland. The Liberals actually won a majority in their own right with 75 seats, the most that the party had ever won. It was only the third time (the others being 1975 and 1977) that the main non-Labor party has been even theoretically able to govern alone since the Coalition's formation. Nevertheless, Howard kept the Nationals in his government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBetts1996_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBetts1996-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Howard entered office with a 45-seat majority—the second-biggest majority in Australian history, only behind Fraser's 55-seat majority in 1975. At the age of 56, he was sworn in as prime minister on 11 March 1996, ending a record 13 years of Coalition opposition.<sup id="cite_ref-NMA_33-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NMA-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> Howard departed from tradition and made his primary residence <a href="/wiki/Kirribilli_House" title="Kirribilli House">Kirribilli House</a> in Sydney rather than <a href="/wiki/The_Lodge_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="The Lodge (Australia)">The Lodge</a> in Canberra.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Early in the term Howard had championed significant new restrictions on gun ownership following the <a href="/wiki/Port_Arthur_massacre_(Australia)" title="Port Arthur massacre (Australia)">Port Arthur massacre</a> in which 35 people had been shot dead. Achieving agreement in the face of immense opposition from within the Coalition and some State governments, was credited with significantly elevating Howard's stature as prime minister despite a backlash from core Coalition rural constituents.<sup id="cite_ref-The_Howard_Years_episode_4_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Howard_Years_episode_4-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Howard's initial silence on the views of <a href="/wiki/Pauline_Hanson" title="Pauline Hanson">Pauline Hanson</a>—a disendorsed Liberal Party candidate and later independent MP from the Brisbane area—was criticised in the press as an endorsement of her views.<sup id="cite_ref-howardyearschronology_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-howardyearschronology-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> When Hanson had made derogatory statements about minorities, Howard not only canceled her Liberal endorsement, but declared she would not be allowed to sit as a Liberal if elected.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> Howard repudiated Hanson's views seven months after her maiden speech.<sup id="cite_ref-howardyearschronology_76-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-howardyearschronology-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following the <a href="/wiki/Wik_Peoples_v_Queensland" title="Wik Peoples v Queensland">Wik Decision</a> of the High Court in 1996, the Howard government moved swiftly to legislate limitations on its possible implications through the so-called <a href="/wiki/Native_Title_Amendment_Act_1998" class="mw-redirect" title="Native Title Amendment Act 1998">Ten-Point Plan</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (March 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:HowardCohen97.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Photograph of Australian Prime Minister John Howard escorted by US Secretary of Defense William Cohen through an armed forces honor cordon into the Pentagon, June 1997" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/HowardCohen97.jpg/220px-HowardCohen97.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="154" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/HowardCohen97.jpg/330px-HowardCohen97.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/HowardCohen97.jpg/440px-HowardCohen97.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2790" data-file-height="1950" /></a><figcaption>Howard and US Secretary of Defense <a href="/wiki/William_Cohen" title="William Cohen">William Cohen</a> in 1997</figcaption></figure>
<p>From 1997, Howard spearheaded the Coalition push to introduce a <a href="/wiki/Goods_and_Services_Tax_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Goods and Services Tax (Australia)">Goods and Services Tax</a> (GST) at the subsequent election; this was despite saying, before winning the prime ministership, that it would "never ever" be part of Coalition policy.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> A long-held conviction of Howard's, his tax reform package was credited with "breaking the circuit" of party morale—boosting his confidence and direction, which had appeared to wane early in the Government's second term.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEErringtonVan_Onselen2007272–273_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEErringtonVan_Onselen2007272–273-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/1998_Australian_federal_election" title="1998 Australian federal election">1998 election</a> was dubbed a "referendum on the GST", and the tax changes—including the GST—were implemented in the government's second term after amendments to the legislation were negotiated with the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Democrats" title="Australian Democrats">Australian Democrats</a> to ensure its passage through the Senate.<sup id="cite_ref-98Election_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98Election-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Through much of its first term, opinion polling was disappointing for the government.<sup id="cite_ref-The_Howard_Years_episode_1_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Howard_Years_episode_1-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> The popularity of Pauline Hanson, and the new restrictions on gun ownership drew many traditionally Coalition voters away from the Howard government. Also unpopular with voters were large spending cuts aimed at eliminating the budget deficit (and Howard's distinction between "core" and "non-core" election promises when cutting spending commitments), <a href="/wiki/Workplace_Relations_Act_1996" title="Workplace Relations Act 1996">industrial changes</a> and the <a href="/wiki/1998_Australian_Waterfront_Dispute" class="mw-redirect" title="1998 Australian Waterfront Dispute">1998 waterfront dispute</a>, the partial sale of government telecommunications company <a href="/wiki/Telstra" title="Telstra">Telstra</a>, and the Government's commitment to a GST.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Howard called a <a href="/wiki/Snap_election" title="Snap election">snap election</a> for October 1998, three months sooner than required. The Coalition actually lost the national two-party preferred vote to Labor, suffering a 14-seat swing. However, the uneven nature of the swing allowed Howard to win a second term in government, with a considerably reduced majority (from 45 seats to 12). Howard himself finished just short of a majority on the first count in his own seat, and was only assured of reelection on the ninth count. He ultimately finished with a fairly comfortable 56 per cent of the two-party preferred vote.<sup id="cite_ref-98Election_80-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98Election-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Second_term">Second term</span></h3>
<p>In 1998, Howard convened a <a href="/wiki/Australian_Constitutional_Convention_1998" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian Constitutional Convention 1998">constitutional convention</a> which decided in principle that Australia should <a href="/wiki/Republicanism_in_Australia" title="Republicanism in Australia">become a republic</a>. At the convention Howard confirmed himself as a monarchist, and said that of the republican options, he preferred the minimalist model. Howard outlined his support for retaining the <a href="/wiki/Monarchy_in_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Monarchy in Australia">Australian constitutional monarchy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> Despite opinion polls suggesting Australians favoured a republic, a <a href="/wiki/1999_Australian_republic_referendum" title="1999 Australian republic referendum">1999 referendum</a> rejected the model chosen by the convention.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> The new <a href="/wiki/President_of_Indonesia" title="President of Indonesia">President of Indonesia</a>, <a href="/wiki/B.J._Habibie" class="mw-redirect" title="B.J. Habibie">B.J. Habibie</a>, had some months earlier agreed to grant special autonomy to <a href="/wiki/Indonesian_occupation_of_East_Timor" title="Indonesian occupation of East Timor">Indonesian-occupied</a> <a href="/wiki/East_Timor" title="East Timor">East Timor</a>. However, following the receipt of a letter sent by Howard to Habibie suggesting that a referendum be held, Habibie made a snap decision to hold a vote on independence.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBlaxland2013143_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlaxland2013143-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> This referendum on the territory's independence triggered a Howard and Downer orchestrated shift in Australian policy. In September 1999, Howard organised an Australian-led international peace-keeping force to East Timor (<a href="/wiki/INTERFET" class="mw-redirect" title="INTERFET">INTERFET</a>), after <a href="/wiki/Pro-Indonesia_militia" title="Pro-Indonesia militia">pro-Indonesia militia</a> launched a violent "scorched-earth" campaign in retaliation to the referendum's overwhelming vote in favour of independence. The successful mission was widely supported by Australian voters, but the government was criticised<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Words_to_watch#Unsupported_attributions" title="Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Words to watch"><span title="The material near this tag possibly uses too-vague attribution or weasel words. (November 2010)">who?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> for "foreign policy failure" following the violence and collapse of diplomatic relations with Indonesia. By Howard's fourth term, relations with Indonesia had recovered to include counter-terrorism cooperation and Australia's $1bn <a href="/wiki/Boxing_Day_Tsunami" class="mw-redirect" title="Boxing Day Tsunami">Boxing Day Tsunami</a> relief efforts, and were assisted by good relations between Howard and Indonesian president <a href="/wiki/Susilo_Bambang_Yudhoyono" title="Susilo Bambang Yudhoyono">Susilo Bambang Yudhoyono</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Throughout his prime-ministership, Howard was resolute in his refusal to provide a parliamentary "apology" to <a href="/wiki/Indigenous_Australians" title="Indigenous Australians">Indigenous Australians</a> as recommended by the 1997 "<a href="/wiki/Bringing_Them_Home" title="Bringing Them Home">Bringing Them Home</a>" Report. Howard made a personal apology before the release of the report.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1999, Howard negotiated a "<a href="/wiki/Motion_of_Reconciliation" title="Motion of Reconciliation">Motion of Reconciliation</a>" with Aboriginal Senator <a href="/wiki/Aden_Ridgeway" title="Aden Ridgeway">Aden Ridgeway</a>. Eschewing use of the word "sorry", the motion recognised mistreatment of Aborigines as the "most blemished chapter" in Australia's history; offered "deep and sincere <i>regret</i>" for past injustices.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> Following his 2007 loss of the prime ministership, Howard was the only living former prime minister who declined to attend the <a href="/wiki/Apology_to_Australia%27s_Indigenous_peoples" title="Apology to Australia&#39;s Indigenous peoples">February 2008 apology</a> made by <a href="/wiki/Kevin_Rudd" title="Kevin Rudd">Kevin Rudd</a> with bi-partisan support.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Howard did not commit to serving a full term if he won the next election; on his 61st birthday in July 2000 he said he would consider the question of retirement when he turned 64.<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> This was interpreted as boosting Costello's leadership aspirations, and the enmity over leadership and succession resurfaced publicly when Howard did not retire at the age of 64.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> In the first half of 2001, rising petrol prices, voter enmity over the implementation of the GST, a spike in inflation and economic slowdown led to bad opinion polls and predictions the Government would lose office in the election later that year.<sup id="cite_ref-wear2001_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wear2001-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> The government announced a series of policy reversals and softenings which boosted the government's fortunes, as did news that the economy had avoided recession. The government's position on "border protection", in particular the <a href="/wiki/Tampa_affair" title="Tampa affair">Tampa affair</a> where Howard refused the landing of asylum seekers rescued by a Norwegian freighter, consolidated the improving polls for the government, as did the <a href="/wiki/September_11_attacks" title="September 11 attacks">11 September 2001 attacks</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Heinemann_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Heinemann-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> Howard led the government to victory in the <a href="/wiki/2001_Australian_federal_election" title="2001 Australian federal election">2001 federal election</a> with an increased majority.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Third_term">Third term</span></h3>
<p>Howard had first met <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_United_States" title="President of the United States">US President</a> <a href="/wiki/George_W._Bush" title="George W. Bush">George W. Bush</a> in the days before the 11 September terrorist attacks and was in Washington the morning of the attacks.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> In response to the attacks, Howard invoked the <a href="/wiki/ANZUS_Treaty" class="mw-redirect" title="ANZUS Treaty">ANZUS Treaty</a>. In October 2001, he committed Australian military personnel to the <a href="/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%93present)" class="mw-redirect" title="War in Afghanistan (2001–present)">War in Afghanistan</a> despite widespread opposition. Howard developed a strong personal relationship with the President,<sup id="cite_ref-NYT_Ally_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NYT_Ally-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> and they shared often similar ideological positions&#160;– including on the role of the United States in world affairs and their approach to the "<a href="/wiki/War_on_Terror" class="mw-redirect" title="War on Terror">War on Terror</a>". In May 2003, Howard made an overnight stay at Bush's <a href="/wiki/Prairie_Chapel_Ranch" title="Prairie Chapel Ranch">Prairie Chapel Ranch</a> in Texas, after which Bush said that Howard "...is not only a man of steel, he's showed the world he's a man of heart."<sup id="cite_ref-SMH+lauds_103-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SMH+lauds-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Bush-Howard_2006_shake.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Photograph of U.S. President George W. Bush shaking hands with Australian Prime Minister John Howard, during the State Arrival Ceremony held for the Prime Minister on the South Lawn of the White House, May 2006" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/30/Bush-Howard_2006_shake.jpg/250px-Bush-Howard_2006_shake.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/30/Bush-Howard_2006_shake.jpg/375px-Bush-Howard_2006_shake.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/30/Bush-Howard_2006_shake.jpg/500px-Bush-Howard_2006_shake.jpg 2x" data-file-width="514" data-file-height="339" /></a><figcaption>Howard maintained a strong friendship with US President <a href="/wiki/George_W._Bush" title="George W. Bush">George W. Bush</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>In April 2002, Howard was the first Australian prime minister to attend a royal funeral, that of <a href="/wiki/Queen_Elizabeth_The_Queen_Mother" title="Queen Elizabeth The Queen Mother">Queen Elizabeth The Queen Mother</a>. In October Howard responded to the <a href="/wiki/2002_Bali_bombing" class="mw-redirect" title="2002 Bali bombing">2002 Bali bombing</a> with calls for solidarity.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> Howard re-dedicated his government to the "<a href="/wiki/War_on_Terror" class="mw-redirect" title="War on Terror">War on Terror</a>".
</p><p>In March 2003, Australia joined the US-led "<a href="/wiki/Multinational_force_in_Iraq" class="mw-redirect" title="Multinational force in Iraq">Multinational force in Iraq</a>" in <a href="/wiki/Australian_contribution_to_the_2003_invasion_of_Iraq" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian contribution to the 2003 invasion of Iraq">sending 2,000 troops and naval units</a> to support in the <a href="/wiki/2003_invasion_of_Iraq" title="2003 invasion of Iraq">invasion of Iraq</a>. In response to the Australian participation in the invasion, there were large protests in Australian cities during March 2003, and Prime Minister Howard was heckled from the public gallery of Parliament House.<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> While opinion polls showed that opposition to the war without UN backing was between 48 and 92 per cent,<sup id="cite_ref-SMH1April2003_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SMH1April2003-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> Howard remained preferred prime-minister over the Leader of the Opposition, <a href="/wiki/Simon_Crean" title="Simon Crean">Simon Crean</a>, although his approval ratings were lower compared to before the war.<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-SMH24Sept2003_108-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SMH24Sept2003-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Throughout 2002 and 2003 Howard had increased his opinion poll lead over Labor Party leader, <a href="/wiki/Simon_Crean" title="Simon Crean">Simon Crean</a>. In December 2003, Crean resigned after losing party support and <a href="/wiki/Mark_Latham" title="Mark Latham">Mark Latham</a> was elected leader. Howard called <a href="/wiki/2004_Australian_federal_election" title="2004 Australian federal election">an election for 9 October 2004</a>. While the government was behind Labor in the opinion polls, Howard himself had a large lead over Latham as preferred prime minister. In the lead up to the election, Howard again did not commit to serving a full term.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> Howard attacked Latham's economic record as Mayor of <a href="/wiki/City_of_Liverpool_(New_South_Wales)" title="City of Liverpool (New South Wales)">Liverpool City Council</a> and attacked Labor's economic history.<sup id="cite_ref-TheAge20040830_PMonInterestRates_110-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TheAge20040830_PMonInterestRates-110">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> The election resulted in a five-seat swing to the Coalition, netting it a majority almost as large as in 1996. It also resulted the first, albeit slim, government majority in the Senate since 1981. For the second time since becoming prime minister, Howard came up short of a majority in the first count for his own seat. He was assured of reelection on the third count, ultimately winning 53.3 per cent of the two-party preferred vote.<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> On 21 December 2004, Howard overtook <a href="/wiki/Bob_Hawke" title="Bob Hawke">Bob Hawke</a> to become the second longest-serving Australian prime minister after <a href="/wiki/Robert_Menzies" title="Robert Menzies">Sir Robert Menzies</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ABC_favourite_112-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ABC_favourite-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Fourth_term">Fourth term</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Vladimir_Putin_with_John_Howard-1.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/be/Vladimir_Putin_with_John_Howard-1.jpg/220px-Vladimir_Putin_with_John_Howard-1.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/be/Vladimir_Putin_with_John_Howard-1.jpg/330px-Vladimir_Putin_with_John_Howard-1.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/be/Vladimir_Putin_with_John_Howard-1.jpg/440px-Vladimir_Putin_with_John_Howard-1.jpg 2x" data-file-width="500" data-file-height="334" /></a><figcaption>Howard with Russian president <a href="/wiki/Vladimir_Putin" title="Vladimir Putin">Vladimir Putin</a> at the 2007 APEC Summit</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 2006, with the government now controlling both houses of parliament for the first time since the Fraser era, industrial relations changes were enacted. Named "<a href="/wiki/WorkChoices" title="WorkChoices">WorkChoices</a>" and championed by Howard, they were intended to fundamentally change the employer-employee relationship. Opposed by a broad trade union campaign and antipathy within the electorate, WorkChoices was subsequently seen as a major factor in the government's 2007 election loss.<sup id="cite_ref-The_Howard_Years_episode_4_72-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Howard_Years_episode_4-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In April 2006, the government announced it had completely paid off the last of $96&#160;billion of Commonwealth net debt inherited when it came to power in 1996.<sup id="cite_ref-CostelloSpeech200604_DebtRepaid_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CostelloSpeech200604_DebtRepaid-115">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> By 2007, Howard had been in office for 11 of the 15 years of consecutive annual growth for the Australian economy. Unemployment had fallen from 8.1% at the start of his term to 4.1% in 2007,<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> and average weekly earnings grew 24.4% in real terms.<sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup>  During his prime ministership, opinion polling consistently showed that a majority of the electorate thought his government were better to handle the economy than the Opposition.<sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Howard_May_2006.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/John_Howard_May_2006.jpg/170px-John_Howard_May_2006.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="258" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/8/80/John_Howard_May_2006.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="204" data-file-height="309" /></a><figcaption>Howard in 2006</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 2006, <a href="/wiki/Ian_McLachlan" title="Ian McLachlan">Ian McLachlan</a> and <a href="/wiki/Peter_Costello" title="Peter Costello">Peter Costello</a> said that under a 1994 deal between Howard and Costello, Howard would serve one and a half terms as prime minister if the Coalition won the next election before stepping aside to allow Costello to take over. Howard denied that this constituted a deal;<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> Citing strong party room support for him as leader, Howard stated later that month that he would remain to contest the 2007 election.<sup id="cite_ref-howards_decision_welcomed_124-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-howards_decision_welcomed-124">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> Six weeks before the election, Howard indicated he would stand down during the next term, and anointed Costello as his successor.<sup id="cite_ref-ABC20070912_HowardOnLeadership_125-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ABC20070912_HowardOnLeadership-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> 
The Coalition trailed Labor in opinion polls from mid-2006 onward, but Howard still consistently led Labor leader <a href="/wiki/Kim_Beazley" title="Kim Beazley">Kim Beazley</a> on the question of preferred prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> In December 2006, after <a href="/wiki/Kevin_Rudd" title="Kevin Rudd">Kevin Rudd</a> became Labor leader, the two-party preferred deficit widened even further and Rudd swiftly overtook Howard as preferred prime minister. Howard chaired <a href="/wiki/APEC_Australia_2007" title="APEC Australia 2007">APEC Australia 2007</a>, culminating in the <a href="/wiki/Asia-Pacific_Economic_Cooperation" title="Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation">APEC</a> Economic Leaders Meeting in Sydney during September.<sup id="cite_ref-DPMC_APEC2007_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DPMC_APEC2007-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> The meeting was at times overshadowed by further leadership speculation following continued poor poll results.<sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In May 2006, the degradation of Aboriginal communities, and the frequent child sexual abuses that occurred within these, was brought to the forefront of the public's mind. In response to this, <a href="/wiki/Little_Children_are_Sacred" title="Little Children are Sacred">a report into child sexual abuse</a> in the Northern Territory was commissioned. Following this, there was an <a href="/wiki/Northern_Territory_National_Emergency_Response" title="Northern Territory National Emergency Response">intervention</a> into these Northern Territory communities. This received widespread criticism, with some holding that it was no more than another attempt to control these communities. Howard was not exempt from this criticism on the grounds of racism.<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Howard supported the Bush administration's <a href="/wiki/Iraq_War_troop_surge_of_2007" title="Iraq War troop surge of 2007">2007 surge strategy in Iraq</a>, and criticised Democrat US presidential candidate <a href="/wiki/Barack_Obama" title="Barack Obama">Barack Obama</a> for calling for a complete withdrawal of Coalition troops by March 2008.<sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="2007_election">2007 election</span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:1699730661_1b1d7843d3_b.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/1699730661_1b1d7843d3_b.jpg/220px-1699730661_1b1d7843d3_b.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/1699730661_1b1d7843d3_b.jpg/330px-1699730661_1b1d7843d3_b.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/1699730661_1b1d7843d3_b.jpg/440px-1699730661_1b1d7843d3_b.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="685" /></a><figcaption>Electioneering balloons from the Liberal and Labor parties in <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Bennelong" title="Division of Bennelong">Bennelong</a> during the <a href="/wiki/2007_Australian_federal_election" title="2007 Australian federal election">2007 federal election</a></figcaption></figure>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/2007_Australian_federal_election" title="2007 Australian federal election">2007 Australian federal election</a></div>
<p>Leading up to the <a href="/wiki/2007_Australian_federal_election" title="2007 Australian federal election">24 November election</a>, the Coalition had been behind Labor in the polls for almost two years, a margin that grew even larger after Rudd became opposition leader. In the election, Howard and his government were defeated, suffering a 23-seat swing to Labor, which was almost as large as the 29-seat swing that propelled him to power in 1996. During the election campaign he was targeted by protesters including the John Howard Ladies Auxiliary Fanclub.<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup>  Howard lost his seat of Bennelong to former journalist <a href="/wiki/Maxine_McKew" title="Maxine McKew">Maxine McKew</a> with 44,685 votes (51.4 per cent) to Howard's 42,251 (48.6 per cent). The latest redistribution placed Bennelong right on the edge of seats Labor needed to win to make Rudd prime minister. The ABC actually listed Bennelong as a Labor gain on election night.<sup id="cite_ref-ABC2007ElectionBennelong_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ABC2007ElectionBennelong-132">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> However, the result remained in doubt for a few days after the election. The final tally indicated that McKew defeated Howard on the 14th count due to a large flow of <a href="/wiki/Australian_Greens" title="Australian Greens">Green</a> preferences to her; 3,793 (78.84 per cent) of Green voters listed McKew as their second preference.<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> Howard was only the second Australian prime minister to lose his seat in an election since <a href="/wiki/Stanley_Bruce" title="Stanley Bruce">Stanley Bruce</a> in 1929.<sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> He remained in office as caretaker prime minister until the formal swearing in of Rudd's government on 3 December.<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Media analysis of The Australian Election Study, a postal survey of 1,873 voters during the 2007 poll, found that although respondents respected Howard and thought he had won the 6-week election campaign, Howard was considered "at odds with public opinion on cut-through issues", his opponent had achieved the highest "likeability" rating in the survey's 20-year history, and a majority had decided their voting intention before the election campaign.<sup id="cite_ref-AUS20080524_AustElectionSurvey_137-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AUS20080524_AustElectionSurvey-137">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Retirement">Retirement</span></h2>
<p>In January 2008, Howard signed with the speaking agency called the Washington Speakers Bureau, joining <a href="/wiki/Tony_Blair" title="Tony Blair">Tony Blair</a>, <a href="/wiki/Colin_Powell" title="Colin Powell">Colin Powell</a>, <a href="/wiki/Madeleine_Albright" title="Madeleine Albright">Madeleine Albright</a>, and others. He was available for two speeches, <i>Leadership in the New Century</i> and <i>The Global Economic Future</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Australian and New Zealand cricket boards unsuccessfully nominated Howard as their candidate for president of the <a href="/wiki/International_Cricket_Council" title="International Cricket Council">International Cricket Council</a> (ICC).<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> Howard was the chairman of the <a href="/wiki/International_Democrat_Union" title="International Democrat Union">International Democrat Union</a> (IDU), a body of international conservative political parties, between 2002 and 2014,<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> when he was succeeded by <a href="/wiki/John_Key" title="John Key">John Key</a> of <a href="/wiki/New_Zealand" title="New Zealand">New Zealand</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> In 2008, he was appointed a director of the foundation established to preserve the legacy of <a href="/wiki/Donald_Bradman#Later_years_and_legacy" class="mw-redirect" title="Donald Bradman">Donald Bradman</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Howard was the subject of a lengthy interview series by <i><a href="/wiki/The_Australian" title="The Australian">The Australian</a></i> columnist <a href="/wiki/Janet_Albrechtsen" title="Janet Albrechtsen">Janet Albrechtsen</a> in 2014, which aired as a featured story on <a href="/wiki/Seven_Network" title="Seven Network">Seven Network</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/Sunday_Night_(Australian_TV_program)" title="Sunday Night (Australian TV program)">Sunday Night</a></i>, and again in January 2015 as its own five-part series on <a href="/wiki/Sky_News_Australia" title="Sky News Australia">Sky News Australia</a> entitled <i>Howard Defined</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-HowardDef_144-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HowardDef-144">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> In November 2017, Howard launched the <a href="/wiki/Paul_Ramsay" title="Paul Ramsay">Ramsay</a> Centre for Western Civilisation, headed by Simon Haines, formerly professor of English at the <a href="/wiki/Chinese_University_of_Hong_Kong" title="Chinese University of Hong Kong">Chinese University of Hong Kong</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> In 2017, Howard endorsed a "No" vote in the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Marriage_Law_Postal_Survey" title="Australian Marriage Law Postal Survey">Australian Marriage Law Postal Survey</a> and joined the campaign against <a href="/wiki/Same-sex_marriage" title="Same-sex marriage">same-sex marriage</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In February 2019, Howard provided a character reference for <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_(Catholic_Church)" title="Cardinal (Catholic Church)">Cardinal</a> <a href="/wiki/George_Pell" title="George Pell">George Pell</a>, a senior leader of the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Australia" title="Catholic Church in Australia">Catholic Church in Australia</a> and former <a href="/wiki/Secretariat_for_the_Economy" title="Secretariat for the Economy">Vatican Treasurer</a>, whose <a href="/wiki/George_Pell#Trial,_conviction_and_acquittal" title="George Pell">conviction</a> on five counts of child sexual abuse while Archbishop of Melbourne was later overturned by the High Court.<sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> Howard's character reference followed Pell's convictions, and was provided along with nine others<sup id="cite_ref-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-151">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> to support Pell's barrister's submissions in the pre-sentencing hearing.<sup id="cite_ref-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-152">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In October 2021, Howard endorsed <a href="/wiki/Dominic_Perrottet" title="Dominic Perrottet">Dominic Perrottet</a> to succeed <a href="/wiki/Gladys_Berejiklian" title="Gladys Berejiklian">Gladys Berejiklian</a> as <a href="/wiki/Premier_of_New_South_Wales" title="Premier of New South Wales">Premier of New South Wales</a> following Berejiklian's resignation as Premier.<sup id="cite_ref-153" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-153">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In July 2023, ahead of the <a href="/wiki/2023_Australian_Indigenous_Voice_referendum" title="2023 Australian Indigenous Voice referendum">Australian Indigenous Voice referendum</a>, Howard said that "the luckiest thing that happened to this country was being colonised by the British. Not that they were perfect by any means, but they were infinitely more successful and beneficent colonisers than other European countries".<sup id="cite_ref-154" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-154">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Honours">Honours</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Howard_bust.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/John_Howard_bust.jpg/170px-John_Howard_bust.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="220" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/John_Howard_bust.jpg/255px-John_Howard_bust.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/John_Howard_bust.jpg/340px-John_Howard_bust.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1153" data-file-height="1489" /></a><figcaption>Bust of John Howard by political cartoonist, caricaturist and sculptor <a href="/wiki/Peter_Nicholson_(cartoonist)" title="Peter Nicholson (cartoonist)">Peter Nicholson</a> located in the <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister%27s_Avenue" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime Minister&#39;s Avenue">Prime Minister's Avenue</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Ballarat_Botanical_Gardens" title="Ballarat Botanical Gardens">Ballarat Botanical Gardens</a></figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Orders">Orders</span></h3>
<ul><li><span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/Australia" title="Australia"><img alt="Australia" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg/23px-Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="12" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg/35px-Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg/46px-Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1280" data-file-height="640" /></a></span></span> 26 January 2008: <a href="/wiki/Companion_of_the_Order_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Companion of the Order of Australia">Companion of the Order of Australia</a> (AC) "for distinguished service to the Parliament of Australia, particularly as prime minister and through contributions to economic and social policy reform, fostering and promoting Australia's interests internationally, and the development of significant philanthropic links between the business sector, arts and charitable organisations".<sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/Royal_Standard_of_Australia_%281962%E2%80%932022%29.svg/23px-Royal_Standard_of_Australia_%281962%E2%80%932022%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="12" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/Royal_Standard_of_Australia_%281962%E2%80%932022%29.svg/35px-Royal_Standard_of_Australia_%281962%E2%80%932022%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/Royal_Standard_of_Australia_%281962%E2%80%932022%29.svg/46px-Royal_Standard_of_Australia_%281962%E2%80%932022%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="300" /></span></span></span> 1 January 2012: <a href="/wiki/Member_of_the_Order_of_Merit" class="mw-redirect" title="Member of the Order of Merit">Member of the Order of Merit</a> (OM) by Queen Elizabeth II<sup id="cite_ref-156" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-156">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Medals">Medals</span></h3>
<ul><li><span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/Australia" title="Australia"><img alt="Australia" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg/23px-Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="12" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg/35px-Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg/46px-Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1280" data-file-height="640" /></a></span></span> 1 January 2001: <a href="/wiki/Centenary_Medal" title="Centenary Medal">Centenary Medal</a><sup id="cite_ref-157" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-157">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>

Bush.<sup id="cite_ref-160" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-160">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-161" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-161">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup></li>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Organisations">Organisations</span></h3>
<ul><li><span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/Australia" title="Australia"><img alt="Australia" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg/23px-Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="12" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg/35px-Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg/46px-Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1280" data-file-height="640" /></a></span></span> 26 January 1997: <a href="/wiki/Australian_Father_of_the_Year_award" title="Australian Father of the Year award">Australian Father of the Year</a></li>
<li><span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/a/a4/Flag_of_the_United_States.svg/23px-Flag_of_the_United_States.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="12" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/a/a4/Flag_of_the_United_States.svg/35px-Flag_of_the_United_States.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/a/a4/Flag_of_the_United_States.svg/46px-Flag_of_the_United_States.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1235" data-file-height="650" /></span></span>&#160;</span><a href="/wiki/United_States" title="United States">United States</a> 22 August 2005: <a href="/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson_Awards" title="Woodrow Wilson Awards">Woodrow Wilson Award</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson_Center" class="mw-redirect" title="Woodrow Wilson Center">Woodrow Wilson Center</a> of the U.S. <a href="/wiki/Smithsonian_Institution" title="Smithsonian Institution">Smithsonian Institution</a></li>
<li><span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/a/a4/Flag_of_the_United_States.svg/23px-Flag_of_the_United_States.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="12" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/a/a4/Flag_of_the_United_States.svg/35px-Flag_of_the_United_States.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/a/a4/Flag_of_the_United_States.svg/46px-Flag_of_the_United_States.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1235" data-file-height="650" /></span></span>&#160;</span><a href="/wiki/United_States" title="United States">United States</a> May 2006: <a href="/wiki/B%27nai_B%27rith#Awards" title="B&#39;nai B&#39;rith">Presidential Gold Medal</a> from the <a href="/wiki/B%27nai_B%27rith_International" class="mw-redirect" title="B&#39;nai B&#39;rith International">B'nai B'rith International</a><sup id="cite_ref-163" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-163">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup></li>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Appointments">Appointments</span></h3>
<ul><li><span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/Flag_of_Israel.svg/21px-Flag_of_Israel.svg.png" decoding="async" width="21" height="15" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/Flag_of_Israel.svg/32px-Flag_of_Israel.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/Flag_of_Israel.svg/41px-Flag_of_Israel.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1100" data-file-height="800" /></span></span>&#160;</span><a href="/wiki/Israel" title="Israel">Israel</a> 30 November 2008&#160;&#8211;&#32;present: <a href="/wiki/Hebrew_University_of_Jerusalem" title="Hebrew University of Jerusalem">Hebrew University of Jerusalem</a>, Honorary Doctorate for "outstanding statesmanship and leading role on the world stage in promoting democracy and combating international terrorism" and his "remarkable understanding of, and exceptional support for, the State of Israel and his deep friendship with the Australian Jewish community".<sup id="cite_ref-mumbai-attacks_168-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-mumbai-attacks-168">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/Flag_of_Queensland.svg/23px-Flag_of_Queensland.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="12" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/Flag_of_Queensland.svg/35px-Flag_of_Queensland.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/Flag_of_Queensland.svg/46px-Flag_of_Queensland.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1200" data-file-height="600" /></span></span></span> 14 February 2009&#160;&#8211;&#32;present: <a href="/wiki/Bond_University" title="Bond University">Bond University</a>, Honorary doctorate<sup id="cite_ref-169" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-169">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/00/Flag_of_New_South_Wales.svg/23px-Flag_of_New_South_Wales.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="12" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/00/Flag_of_New_South_Wales.svg/35px-Flag_of_New_South_Wales.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/00/Flag_of_New_South_Wales.svg/46px-Flag_of_New_South_Wales.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1200" data-file-height="600" /></span></span></span> 10 April 2012&#160;&#8211;&#32;present: <a href="/wiki/Macquarie_University" title="Macquarie University">Macquarie University</a>, Honorary Doctor of Letters<sup id="cite_ref-170" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-170">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup></li>
